**Starship** is a [two-stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-stage), [fully reusable](336014.md), [super heavy-lift launch vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_heavy-lift_launch_vehicle) under development by American aerospace company [SpaceX](832774.md). Currently built and launched from [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase) in Texas, it is intended as the successor to the company's [Falcon 9](2647515.md) and [Falcon Heavy](31406060.md) rockets,[^cite_note-8] and is part of SpaceX's broader [reusable launch system development program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_reusable_launch_system_development_program). If completed as designed, Starship would be the first fully reusable orbital rocket and have the [highest payload capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_heavy-lift_launch_vehicle#Comparison) of any launch vehicle to date. As of September 28, 2026, Starship [has launched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_launches) 14 times, with 9 successful flights and 5 failures.

The vehicle consists of two stages: the [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy) booster and the [Starship spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)), both powered by [Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor) engines burning [liquid methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_methane) (the main component of [natural gas](22131.md)) and [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen). Both stages are intended to return to the launch site and [land vertically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTVL) at the launch tower for potential reuse.[^cite_note-MH-9] Once in space, the Starship upper stage is intended to function as a standalone spacecraft capable of carrying [crew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_spaceflight) and [cargo](184527.md).[^cite_note-10] Missions beyond [low Earth orbit](47568.md) would require multiple in-orbit [refueling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_propellant_depot) flights. At the end of its mission, Starship [reenters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry) the atmosphere using [heat shield tiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_reentry#Thermal_protection_systems) similar to those of the [Space Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle).[^cite_note-Reichhardt-2021-11] SpaceX states that its goal is to reduce launch costs by reusing and [mass producing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production) both stages.

SpaceX initially expressed ambitions to use Starship for [crewed missions to Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program),[^cite_note-12] but the plan was revised to prioritize returning [humans to the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing).[^cite_note-13] SpaceX has also proposed a wide range of missions for Starship, such as deploying large satellites, [space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_station) modules,[^cite_note-space20230510-14] and [space telescopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescopes).[^cite_note-15] A crewed variant, the [Starship Human Landing System](64020556.md), is being developed under a contract with [NASA](18426568.md) as part of the [Artemis program](60758751.md), with a docking test as part of [Artemis III](50493439.md), currently scheduled for 2027,[^cite_note-NASA-2023-16] and a crewed lunar landing scheduled for 2028.[^cite_note-Ars-Berger-Artemis-17]

SpaceX began [developing concepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_design_history) for a super heavy-lift reusable launch vehicle as early as 2005. Starship's current design and name were introduced in 2018. Development has followed an [iterative and incremental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iterative_and_incremental_development) approach, involving a high number of prototype vehicles and test flights. The first full Starship rocket was [launched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1) April 20, 2023, exploding four minutes after liftoff.[^cite_note-18] The program has failed to meet many of its optimistic schedule goals, with several setbacks in development, including the failure of the first four Block 2 upper stages in 2025.[^cite_note-19] However in September 2026, [Starship flight 14](78384681.md) achieved orbit of the Starship spacecraft in a significant milestone, despite an engine failure.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]

## Description

When stacked and fully fueled, Starship has a mass of approximately 5,300 t (11,700,000 lb),[^cite_note-24] a diameter of 9 m (30 ft)[^cite_note-25] and a height of 121.3 m (398 ft).[^cite_note-Berger_blocks-26] The rocket has been designed with the goal of being fully [reusable](336014.md) to reduce launch costs;[^cite_note-Inman-2021-27] it consists of the [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy) [booster](373603.md) and the [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)) upper stage[^cite_note-Amos-2021-28] which are powered by [Raptor (RSL) and Raptor Vacuum (RVac) engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor).[^cite_note-cnet-20211021-29]

The bodies of both rocket stages are made from [stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stainless_steel)[^cite_note-slashgear-20220923-30] and are manufactured by stacking and welding stainless steel cylinders.[^cite_note-Berger-2020b-31] These cylinders have a height of 1.83 m (6 ft), and a thickness of 3.97 mm (0.156 in).[^cite_note-Berger-2020b-31]

A common dome inside the spacecraft separate the methane and oxygen tanks.[^cite_note-Berger-2020b-31] SpaceX has stated that Starship, in its "baseline reusable design", will have a payload capacity of 100–150 t (220,000–331,000 lb) to [low Earth orbit](47568.md) and 27 t (60,000 lb) to [geostationary transfer orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geostationary_transfer_orbit).[^cite_note-SpaceX-users-guide-32]

### Super Heavy booster

Main article: [SpaceX Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy)

Super Heavy was initially 71 m (233 ft) tall (in its Block 1 and Block 2 design, now retired), while Block 3 Super Heavy is 72.3 meters tall.[^cite_note-Berger_2024-33] It is 9 m (30 ft) wide,[^cite_note-Berger_2024-33] and is composed of four general sections, in ascending order: the engines, the [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen) tank, the [liquid methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_methane) tank, and the interstage.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021sh-34]

### Tanks

The two cryogenic propellant tanks on Super Heavy are separated by a common bulkhead, a similar structural design to the [S-II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II) and [S-IVB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IVB) stages on the [Saturn V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_V) rocket.[^cite_note-Building_SpaceX_2023-35] After [Starship's second flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2), the common dome's design was changed to be more elliptical,[^cite_note-Everyday_Astronaut_2025-36] altering the propellant capacity of both tanks by a small amount.[^cite_note-Everyday_Astronaut_2025-36] Each tank possesses roughly 74 stringers for structural reinforcement, attached to their interior walls.[^cite_note-37] The booster's two tanks hold a combined 3,400 t (7,500,000 lb) of propellant:[^cite_note-spacex-homepage-38] 2,700 t (6,000,000 lb) of liquid oxygen and 700 t (1,500,000 lb) of liquid methane.[^cite_note-39] Fuel is fed to the engines via a single liquid downcomer, and channeled into distribution [manifolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifold_\(fluid_mechanics\)) of the engines.[^cite_note-Building_SpaceX_2023-35] This system was upgraded on Block 3 boosters, featuring a substantially larger transfer tube connecting the engines and the methane tank.[^cite_note-40] Block 1 booster's have a single booster quick disconnect, along with multiple quick disconnects for the outer engines, while Block 3 boosters have two quick disconnects.[^cite_note-B3-41] One disconnect feeds liquid oxygen into the vehicle, the other feeds liquid methane.[^cite_note-B3-41]

The oxygen tank ends at the thrust puck of the vehicle. While the outer twenty engines are mounted on a ring attached to the first steel ring, the inner thirteen are mounted onto the thrust puck, a part of the aft dome.[^cite_note-Building_SpaceX_2023-35] Large steel structures are attached to the bottom of the dome, reinforcing the puck sufficiently to fully support the inner thirteen engines, and at the same time providing pathways for methane and oxygen into the engines.[^cite_note-Building_SpaceX_2023-35] In addition, large filters were added in this region beginning on Booster 10.[^cite_note-spacex-ft-2-42][^cite_note-fuel-contamination-43] Liquid oxygen is supplied by a header tank during landing burn for the inner thirteen engines.[^cite_note-RI-44] On Booster 15, the header tank had at least nine additional tanks attached, increasing capacity for the landing burn.[^cite_note-fuel-contamination-43] The added tanks may have been present on Boosters 12, 13, and 14, though this was unconfirmed as of February 2025.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight_2024d-45] Booster 5 was the only 29-engine booster to receive a header tank, mounted to the side of the oxygen tank instead of being integrated with the thrust puck.[^cite_note-RI-44]

The methane funnel is partially contained within the header tank, as the methane sump is directly below it.[^cite_note-RI-44] On Booster 7 and all subsequent vehicles, four aerodynamic [chines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chine_\(aeronautics\)) are located on the outside of the oxygen tank, providing aerodynamic lift during descent,[^cite_note-IS-46] as well as housing batteries, [composite overwrapped pressure vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_overwrapped_pressure_vessel) (COPVs) for spin start,[^cite_note-47] and CO<sub>2</sub> tanks for [fire suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_fire_suppression_system).[^cite_note-Building_SpaceX_2023-35][^cite_note-MU-48] On vehicles with hydraulic power units (HPUs), COPVs dedicated to engine ignition, batteries, and communication antennae were located within the HPU cover instead of the chines.[^cite_note-MU-48]

### Propulsion

Super Heavy is powered by 33 [Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor) engines, which on Block 1 vehicles are housed within a dedicated shielding compartment.[^cite_note-MU-48] SpaceX upgraded the engines for their first Starship flight of 2026 from the Raptor 2 variant to the Raptor 3 variant. This compartment is not present before engine installation, thus boosters are roughly three meters shorter prior to engine installation.[^cite_note-musk20210704-49] The outer 20 engines, arranged in a ring, are fixed in place.[^cite_note-50] To save weight, the 20 outer Raptor 2 engines are started using [ground support equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_support_equipment) on the launch mount and cannot be reignited for subsequent burns.[^cite_note-Bergin-2021-51] The outer 20 Raptor 3's are able to relight, meaning all Raptor 3 engines can relight. For Raptor 2, the inner thirteen engines are equipped with [gimbal actuators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbaled_thrust) and reignite for the boostback and landing burns.[^cite_note-52] On Raptor 3, all engines relight for boostback. After [Starship's first flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1), this gimbaling system was switched from a hydraulic system to an electric one, enabling the removal of the hydraulic power units.[^cite_note-spacex-ft-2-42] This change was made to the upper stage after the [second flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2). During the ascent and boostback burns, the engines draw propellant from the main tanks, with the liquid oxygen being drawn from a dedicated header tank during the landing burn.[^cite_note-IS-46] Like the thrust vector control system, the engine shielding, which isolates individual engines in the event of a failure, was upgraded after Starship's first flight test, alongside the [fire suppression system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_fire_suppression_system).[^cite_note-spacex-ft-2-42] The aft bay has eighteen vents visible on the outside of the booster, which are believed to be connected to the outer 20 engines,[^cite_note-MU-48] while the center engines vent directly below the launch pad.[^cite_note-MU-48]

The [Raptor engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor) uses a [full-flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion) staged combustion cycle with oxygen and methane-rich turbopumps.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] Before 2014, only two full-flow staged-combustion rocket engine designs had advanced enough to undergo testing: the Soviet [RD-270](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-270) project in the 1960s and the [Aerojet Rocketdyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerojet_Rocketdyne) [Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Powerhead_Demonstrator) in the mid-2000s.[^cite_note-nsf20140307sh-55] To improve performance, the engines burn subcooled propellant; i.e. the propellants are cooled below their respective boiling points to further increase their density and the engine mass flow rates.[^cite_note-spacex-homepage-38]

The Block 1 version of the booster (used through November 2024) produced a total of 73.5 MN (16,500,000 lb<sub>f</sub>)[^cite_note-musk-v-overview-56] just over twice that of the NASA [Saturn V first stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IC),[^cite_note-SICMaxThrust-57] with this total being expected to increase to 80.8 MN (18,200,000 lb<sub>f</sub>) for Block 3 boosters and later up to 98.1 MN (22,100,000 lb<sub>f</sub>) with the Block 4 vehicle.[^cite_note-Berger_2024-33] The thirty three engines collectively produce large [shock diamonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_diamond) in the exhaust jet, visible during ascent and descent.[^cite_note-58]

During unpowered flight in the upper atmosphere, [control authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft_attitude_control) is provided by [cold gas thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_gas_thruster) fed with residual [ullage gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullage).[^cite_note-RI-44] Four perpendicular vents are located within the interstage.[^cite_note-MU-48] Additional vents are located just below the common dome, pointing down toward the engines at a slight angle.[^cite_note-MU-48]

The Block 3 booster contains an updated aft dome, with metallic heat shield tiles mounted upon it.[^cite_note-59]

### Interstage

&#x20;The interstage is equipped with four electrically actuated [grid fins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid_fin) made of stainless steel, each with a mass of roughly 3 t (6,600 lb).[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021sh-34] The fins remain extended during ascent to save weight,[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021sh-34][^cite_note-60] though this results in mild warping during stage separation.[^cite_note-61] The interstage also has protruding [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint), located between grid fins, allowing the booster to be lifted or caught by the launch tower, nicknamed "Mechazilla".[^cite_note-Weber-2021sh-62][^cite_note-\:0-63] The ability to lift a booster from these hardpoints was proven on August 23, 2022, when Booster 7 was lifted onto OLM A.[^cite_note-64] The first catch of a booster occurred on October 13, 2024, using Booster 12.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight_2024c-65]

Animation of Super Heavy's integration to the launch mount, using mechanical arms

After the [first Starship test flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_integrated_flight_test_1), all boosters have an additional 1.8 m[^cite_note-Wang_2020-66] tall vented interstage to enable [hot staging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_staging).[^cite_note-Wall-23-08-67] During hot staging, Super Heavy shuts down all but the three center engines,[^cite_note-Skibba-1059-68][^cite_note-nsf-23-06-24-69] while the second stage fires its engines before separating, thus the second stage "pushes off" from the first stage, giving added thrust.[^cite_note-Skibba-1059-68] The vented interstage contains a dome to shield the top of Super Heavy from the second stage's exhaust.[^cite_note-Wall-23-08-67][^cite_note-nsf-23-06-24-69] Elon Musk in 2023 claimed that this change might result in a 10% increase in the payload to [low Earth orbit](47568.md).[^cite_note-nsf-23-06-24-69] Beginning with Booster 11, the vented interstage is jettisoned after completion of the boostback burn, to reduce mass during descent.[^cite_note-STARSHIP_2024-70] As of December 2025, SpaceX does not intend to jettison the interstage when flying Block 3 boosters, as the vented section will be directly integrated into the vehicle.[^cite_note-spx20250813-71]

On Block 3 boosters, the interstage is directly integrated into the methane tank, and the number of grid fins is reduced from four to three, in a 90/90/180 degree arrangement.[^cite_note-B3-41] These grid fins are roughly one and a half times the size of the Block 1 and 2 grid fins,[^cite_note-spx20250813-71] and are positioned lower on the vehicle. According to SpaceX, the repositioning reduces the heat experienced during stage separation. Additionally, the fins are integrated with the catch pins.[^cite_note-72] The grids in the fin without an opposite are slightly angled to offset the [asymmetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asymmetry).

### Starship spacecraft

Main article: [SpaceX Starship (spacecraft)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\))



Diagram of a Block 1 Starship's internal structure. Not shown in this diagram are the flaps: the aft flaps are placed at the bottom (or left in this orientation), and the forward flaps are placed at the top (here, right) portion of the vehicle.

The now retired Block 2 version of Starship was 52.1 m (171 ft) tall, 9 m (30 ft) wide,[^cite_note-Berger_blocks-26] and was composed of four general sections: the engine bay, the oxygen tank, the fuel tank, and the payload bay.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021-22] The retired Block 1 was constructed in a similar manner, though it was only 50.3 m (165 ft) tall. [Elon Musk](909036.md) stated in 2021 that the vehicle had a [dry mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_weight) of roughly 100 t (220,000 lb).[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021-22] The windward side is protected by a [heat shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_shield) composed of eighteen thousand[^cite_note-Sheetz-2021a2-73] hexagonal black tiles that can withstand temperatures of 1,400 °C (2,600 °F).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-Reichhardt-2021-11] It is designed to protect the vehicle during [atmospheric entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry) and is to be used multiple times with minimal maintenance between flights.[^cite_note-Inman-20212-75] The [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide)-based tiles are attached to Starship with pins,[^cite_note-Reichhardt-2021-11] and had small gaps in between to allow for [heat expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_expansion).[^cite_note-Bergeron-2021-12-76] After [flight test 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4), SpaceX added a secondary ablative layer under the primary heat shield,[^cite_note-77] though this was only added to the flaps of the [flight test 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6) vehicle.[^cite_note-78] This ablative layer is likely composed of pyron, which is similar in composition to [carbon composites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon-fiber_reinforced_polymer).[^cite_note-nsf-flight-5-79] The total mass of the heat shield and ablative layer of a Block 1 ship is 10.5 t (23,000 lb).[^cite_note-80] After [flight test 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10), SpaceX added a felt, called "crunch wrap," between the gaps in between the tiles to prevent heat seeping in.

### Tanks

The propellant tanks on Starship are separated by a common bulkhead, similar to the ones used on the [S-II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II) and [S-IVB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-IVB) stages on the [Saturn V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn_V) rocket.[^cite_note-81] While Block 2 vehicles use an elliptical dome,[^cite_note-\:14-82] the common and forward domes of the Block 1 design were more conical.[^cite_note-\:12-83] Block 1 vehicles only had 24 [stringers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longeron) within the oxygen tank,[^cite_note-84] while Block 2 vehicles had these added to the methane tanks.[^cite_note-\:14-82] The vehicle's tanks hold 1,500 t (3,300,000 lb) of propellant,[^cite_note-Berger_blocks-26] consisting of 1,170 t (2,580,000 lb) of liquid oxygen and 330 t (730,000 lb) of liquid methane.[^cite_note-85]

Fuel is fed to the engines via four downcomers, with three smaller downcomers feeding the [Vacuum Raptors/RVacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor#Raptor_Vacuum) and the central downcomer feeding the inner three engines.[^cite_note-\:14-82] The central downcomer connects to a large sump, instead of directly to the methane tank.[^cite_note-86] The original design had a single downcomer.[^cite_note-\:10-87] The liquid oxygen (LOX) downcomer extends into the LOX tank, with a small expanded portion of unknown purpose.[^cite_note-88] Two additional downcomers route methane and oxygen from the header tanks.[^cite_note-89] A camera is located on the walls of the tank, pointed towards the payload bay.[^cite_note-\:09-90]

The oxygen tank terminates with the thrust structure of the vehicle.[^cite_note-\:12-83] The RVac engines are mounted directly to the aft dome, which has reinforcements mounted inside of the tank.[^cite_note-\:12-83] The three sea level engines are mounted on the thrust puck, which forms the bottom of the aft dome.[^cite_note-91] A conical steel structure is mounted inside the bottom of the dome, reinforcing the thrust puck enough to enable its support of the inner three engines.[^cite_note-92] The propellant lines on the vehicles are [vacuum jacketed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-layer_insulation), reducing boiloff while in orbit.[^cite_note-\:10-87]

### Propulsion

Starship is powered by six [Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor) engines, which are housed within a dedicated shielding compartment.[^cite_note-93] Blocks 1 through 3 have three sea-level engines, as well as three engines optimized for operation in the vacuum of space, called RVacs.[^cite_note-nsf-block-3-94] Block 4 ships are expected to have three additional RVacs.[^cite_note-nsf-block-3-94] The sea-level engines are equipped with [gimbal actuators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbaled_thrust), and reignite for the landing burns.[^cite_note-nsf-flight-5-79] After [Starship's second flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2), this gimbaling system was switched from a hydraulic system to an electric one, enabling the removal of the hydraulic power units.[^cite_note-Spacex202502-95] This change was made to the booster after the [first flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1).[^cite_note-Spacex202502-95] There are four engine chill lines onboard the vehicle, though two of these lines may serve another purpose.[^cite_note-\:16-96]

Each engine is protected by a dedicated shielding compartment.[^cite_note-\:16-96] Beginning with S25, the Block 1 design had between 14 and 16 such vents.[^cite_note-\:16-96] Additional vents were added after flight 7.[^cite_note-97] The [fire suppression system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_fire_suppression_system), which uses gaseous nitrogen to purge the engine bay during flight, was upgraded after flight 7.[^cite_note-98] A similar system on the booster uses carbon dioxide to purge the individual engine compartments during flight and static fires.[^cite_note-Spacex202502-95]

The Raptor engine uses a [full-flow staged combustion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion) cycle, which has oxygen and methane-rich turbopumps.[^cite_note-99] Before 2014, only two full-flow staged-combustion rocket engine designs had advanced enough to undergo testing: the Soviet [RD-270](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-270) project in the 1960s and the Aerojet Rocketdyne [Integrated Powerhead Demonstrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Powerhead_Demonstrator) in the mid-2000s.[^cite_note-nsf20140307-100] To improve performance, the engines burn [supercooled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercooling) propellant.[^cite_note-StarshipHomepage2-101]

The Block 1 version of the ship (used through November 2024) produced a total of 12.25 MN (2,750,000 lb<sub>f</sub>)[^cite_note-Berger_blocks-26] almost triple the thrust of the [Saturn V second stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-II), with this being expected to increase to 15.69 MN (3,530,000 lb<sub>f</sub>) for Block 2 boosters and later up to 26.48 MN (5,950,000 lb<sub>f</sub>) with the Block 3 vehicle.[^cite_note-Berger_blocks-26]

During unpowered flight in orbit, [control authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacecraft_attitude_control) is provided by [cold gas thrusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_gas_thruster) fed with residual [ullage gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullage).[^cite_note-\:12-83] Four such thrusters are located just below the payload bay,[^cite_note-102] and two on the oxygen tank.[^cite_note-nsf20220331-103] Near the top of the nosecone, there are two vents connected to the header tanks.[^cite_note-nsf20220331-103] Additional vents were added at the base of the vehicle after flight two.[^cite_note-104]

### Payload bay

The payload bay hosts the nosecone, header tanks, forward flaps, multiple COPVs, and the so-called "[PEZ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PEZ) dispenser".[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight2025-02-105] The header tanks are mounted at the tip of the payload bay.[^cite_note-\:12-83] The LOX header tank forms the top of the nosecone, with the methane header tank attached directly below it.[^cite_note-nsf-manufacturing-106] These tanks terminate in a conical sump, which are attached to the downcomers.[^cite_note-107] Several COPVs are mounted in the space around the methane header tank, providing the startup gas for the engines,[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight2025-02-105] with twelve additional COPVs within the base of the payload bay.[^cite_note-108]

The nosecone has substantial internal reinforcement, mainly around the forward flap attachment points and lifting points for the chopsticks.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight2025-02-105] The number of internal stringers was increased between Block 1 and Block 2 vehicles.[^cite_note-rw-s33-nose-109] Additional reinforcements are used to support the PEZ dispenser on ships equipped with one.[^cite_note-rw-s33-nose-109] Four Starlink antennas are located within the nosecone.[^cite_note-\:26-110]

The PEZ dispenser is used to deploy Starlink satellites into LEO.[^cite_note-\:12-83] It was first added to S24, though it was permanently sealed until [flight 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3). It consists of the dispenser mechanism and the door.[^cite_note-\:12-83] The door opens by folding into the payload bay.[^cite_note-111]

The dispenser itself is mounted directly to the forward dome.[^cite_note-\:52-112] It has a truss structure for its base, with solid steel used elsewhere.[^cite_note-\:52-112] A mobile track is used in the base, enabling the dispenser to push the satellite out of the vehicle.[^cite_note-\:52-112] After dispensing a satellite, the next payload is lowered onto the base, and is deployed.[^cite_note-\:52-112] The opposite occurs during loading, with the dispenser raising its payloads to receive another satellite.[^cite_note-\:52-112] In order to prevent the satellite from floating out of the mechanism during zero-g operations, the dispenser locks the satellites in position using a "retention frame". This is lowered alongside the satellites during operation.[^cite_note-\:52-112]

### Flaps



Forward flap on a suborbital Starship prototype

Starship controls its reentry with four flaps, two aft flaps mounted to the sides of the engine bay and LOX tank and two forward flaps on the payload bay.[^cite_note-\:11-113] Substantial reinforcements are present in the nosecone for the support of the forward flaps.[^cite_note-\:14-82] According to SpaceX, the flaps replace the need for wings or [tailplane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailplane), reduce the fuel needed for landing, and allow landing at destinations in the [Solar System](26903.md) where runways do not exist (for example, Mars). The flap's hinges are sealed in [aerocovers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeroshell) because they would otherwise be easily damaged during reentry.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021-22] Static wicks are present on the flaps, aiding in the discharge of static electricity.[^cite_note-\:26-110]

Despite this, damage to the forward flaps was observed on flights [four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4),[^cite_note-\:11-113] [five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5),[^cite_note-nsf-flight-5-79] and [six](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6),[^cite_note-114] with near complete loss occurring on flight 4.[^cite_note-115] Beginning with Block 2, the design of these forward flaps was significantly changed, moving leeward and becoming thinner and angled.[^cite_note-\:09-90] This sets them at an approximately 140-degree angle, compared to the 180-degree angle of the aft flaps and previous version of the forward flaps.[^cite_note-\:10-87] This change was made to prevent the static aero from creating a tendency for the Ship to pitch up, even when the forward flaps were stowed,[^cite_note-116] and also reduces the heating on the static aero and forward flaps observed on the last three flights of the Block 1 ship.[^cite_note-eda20240622-117] Both sets of flaps feature cameras in their hinges.[^cite_note-\:09-90]

### Raptor engine

Main article: [SpaceX Raptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor)

A rocket engine with nozzle and intricate plumbing

Sea level-optimized Raptor 1 engine, May 2020

Raptor is a family of rocket engines developed by SpaceX for use in [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)) and [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_\(booster_rocket\)) vehicles. It burns [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen) and [methane](18582230.md) in an efficient and complex [full-flow staged combustion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full-flow_staged_combustion) power cycle. The Raptor engine uses methane as fuel rather than kerosene because methane gives higher performance and prevents the build-up of deposits in the engine from [coking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coking).[^cite_note-O'Callaghan-2019-118] Methane can also be produced from carbon dioxide and hydrogen using the [Sabatier reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_reaction).[^cite_note-Sommerlad-2021-119] The engines are designed to be reused many times with little maintenance.[^cite_note-120]

Raptor operates with an [oxygen-to-methane mixture ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_propellant#Mixture_ratio) of about 3.6\:1, lower than the [stoichiometric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoichiometry) mixture ratio of 4\:1 necessary for complete combustion, since operating at higher temperatures would melt the engine.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021-22] The propellants leave the [pre-burners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preburner) and get injected into the main combustion chamber as hot gases instead of liquid droplets, enabling a higher [power density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_density) as the propellants mix rapidly via [diffusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_diffusion).[^cite_note-O'Callaghan-2019-118] The methane and oxygen are at high enough temperatures and pressures that they [ignite on contact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypergolic_propellant), eliminating the need for igniters in the main combustion chamber.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2022-121] The engine structure itself is mostly aluminum, copper, and steel; oxidizer-side turbopumps and manifolds subject to corrosive oxygen-rich flames are made of an [Inconel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inconel)-like [SX500 superalloy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SX500_\(superalloy\)).[^cite_note-Sesnic-2022-121] Some components are [3D printed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D-printed).[^cite_note-122]

A Raptor 2 engine produces 2.3 [MN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton_\(unit\)) (520,000 lbf) at a [specific impulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Specific_impulse) of 327 seconds (3.21 km/s) at [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level) and 350 seconds (3.4 km/s) in a vacuum.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2022-121] [Raptor vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_vacuum), used on the Starship upper stage, is modified with a regeneratively cooled [nozzle extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nozzle_extension) made of brazed steel tubes, increasing its expansion ratio to about 90 and its specific impulse in vacuum to 380 seconds (3.7 km/s).[^cite_note-Sesnic-2021-22] The main [combustion chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combustion_chamber) operates at a pressure of 350 bar (5,100 psi) exceeding that of any prior operational rocket engine.[^cite_note-O'Callaghan-2019-118] The Raptor's [gimbaling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbal) range is 15°, higher than the [RS-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RS-25)'s 12.5° and the Merlin's 5°. SpaceX has stated they aim to achieve a per unit production cost of US\$250,000 upon starting mass production.[^cite_note-Sesnic-2022-121]

## Versions

| Block | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Payload to orbit (t) | 15 | 35 | 100 | 200 |
| Booster propellant (t) | 3,250 | 3,650 | 4,050 |  |
| Ship propellant (t) | 1,200 | 1,500 | 1,600 | 2,300 |
| Booster liftoff thrust (tf) | 7,500[^cite_note-ift6-musk-6] | 8,240 | 10,000 |  |
| Ship initial thrust (tf) | 1,250 | 1,400 | 1,600 | 2,700 |
| Ship sea-level engines | 3 |  |  |  |
| Ship vacuum engines | 3 | 6 |  |  |
| Booster height (m) | 71 | 72.3 | 81 |  |
| Ship height (m) | 50.3 | 52.1 | 61 |  |
| Total height (m) | 121.3 | 123.3 | 124.4 | 142 |

On April 4, 2024, [Elon Musk](909036.md) provided an update on Starship at [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), where two new versions of Starship were announced, Block 2 and Block 3,[^cite_note-124] intended to address the shortcomings of the pre-production prototypes.

### Block 1

Block 1 was used after [2020–2021 flight tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_upper_stage_flight_tests), beginning with [flight test 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1) at the start of 2023, to [flight tests 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5) and [6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6), then retired.[^cite_note-125]

### Block 2

Block 2 for both stages was used beginning with [flight test 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7) at the start of 2025.[^cite_note-Clark_Block_2-126] Block 2 upper stage vehicles featured a thinner forward flap design, flaps that are positioned more leeward, a 25% increase in propellant capacity, redesigned avionics,[^cite_note-127] two raceways,[^cite_note-128] and an increase in thrust.[^cite_note-RGV_Aerial-2024-129] The integrated vehicle is 3.1 m (10 ft) taller than the previous Block 1 vehicle and was planned to have a payload capacity of at least 100 tons to orbit when reused[^cite_note-RGV_Aerial-2024-129] (similar to the original design before the Block 2 rework) but was retired before an orbital flight, with the final estimate of 35 t of payload to orbit. Additionally, Block 2 vehicles were planned to use Raptor 3 engines, removing the need for secondary engine shielding.[^cite_note-130] However, the first Block 2 vehicle, S33, received upgraded Raptor 2 engines,[^cite_note-131] with an unknown increase in thrust.[^cite_note-132] The Block 2 ship and booster first flew on flight test 7.[^cite_note-133] A [total of six block 2 vehicles were produced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#Development).[^cite_note-NSF-Flight9-134] After [flight test 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11), Block 2 was retired.

### Block 3

Block 3 ships have an improved tile design,[^cite_note-135] [Raptor 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Raptor#Raptor_3) engines,[^cite_note-FS-136] as well as hardware for refueling operations LEO, such as docking ports and a redesigned quick disconnect.[^cite_note-FS-136] The switch to Raptor 3 also enables the removal of most of the aft section's shielding.[^cite_note-FS-136]

Block 3 boosters have an integrated vented interstage/forward dome, three grid fins instead of the prior four, and use the grid fins as tower catch points, eliminating the need for separate catch point structure.[^cite_note-B3-41] Like with the ship, the Block 3 boosters use Raptor 3 engines, allowing for the removal of the majority of the booster's engine shielding, reducing weight by over a ton per engine.[^cite_note-B3-41] First test flights of Block 3 ships began in 2026.

## Planned launch and landing profile



Super Heavy Booster 12 approaching the tower during [Starship flight test 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5) on October 13, 2024

Payloads will be integrated into Starship at a separate facility and then rolled out to the [launch site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceport).[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137] [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy) and [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)) are then to be stacked onto their launch mount and loaded with fuel via the ship quick disconnect (SQD) arm and booster quick disconnect (BQD).[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The SQD and BQD retract, all 33 engines of Super Heavy ignite, and the rocket lifts off.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138]

At approximately 141 seconds after launch[^cite_note-Moon-2022-139] at an altitude of roughly 64 km (40 mi), Super Heavy cuts off 28 of its 33 engines, leaving 5 engines running at reduced throttle.[^cite_note-140]<sup>: 58 </sup> Starship then ignites its engines while still attached to the booster, and separates.[^cite_note-Skibba-1059-68] The booster then rotates to an aft-first attitude, before relighting the 28 other engines for a "boostback burn"[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023b-141] which stops all forward velocity, then switching to 13 engines and finally 3 to precisely target the landing area. After the boostback burn, the booster's engines shut off with Super Heavy on a trajectory for a controlled descent to the launch site using its three grid fins for minor course corrections. Roughly six minutes after launch, shortly before landing,[^cite_note-142] it ignites its inner 13 engines, then shuts off all but the same 5 used for hot-staging,[^cite_note-SpaceX-2024a-143] to perform a [landing burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTVL) which slows it sufficiently to be finally caught by a pair of [electric actuating arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actuator#Electric) attached to the launch tower.[^cite_note-MH-9] The booster landing and catch was successfully demonstrated for the first time on October 13, 2024, with the landing of Booster 12, and twice more with Boosters 14 and 15.[^cite_note-Clark-catch-144]

Meanwhile, the Starship spacecraft continues accelerating to orbital velocity with its six Raptor engines.[^cite_note-145] Once in orbit, the spacecraft is planned to be able to be refueled by another Starship tanker variant.[^cite_note-O'Callaghan-2021-146] Musk has estimated that 8 launches would be needed to refuel a Starship in low Earth orbit completely.[^cite_note-147] NASA has estimated that 16 launches in short succession (due to cryogenic propellant boil-off) would be needed to refuel Starship for one lunar landing partially.[^cite_note-Foust-2023-148] To land on bodies without an atmosphere, such as the Moon, Starship will fire its engines to slow down.[^cite_note-Foust-2021-149] To land on bodies with an atmosphere, such as the Earth and Mars, Starship first slows by entering the atmosphere using a [heat shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_shield).[^cite_note-Inman-2021-27] The spacecraft would then perform a "belly-flop" maneuver by diving through the atmosphere at a 60° angle to the ground,[^cite_note-Chang-2019b-150] controlling its fall using four flaps at the front and aft of the spacecraft.[^cite_note-20201210cnn-tech-151] Shortly before landing, the Raptor engines fire,[^cite_note-20201210cnn-tech-151] using fuel from the header tanks,[^cite_note-Kooser-2019-152] to perform a "landing flip" maneuver to return to a vertical orientation, with the Raptor engines' gimbaling helping to maneuver the craft.[^cite_note-20201210cnn-tech-151] Omitting the [thermal protection system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry), flaps, and tower-catch hardware for a guided atmospheric deceleration and "Mechazilla" tower catch allows for significant weight savings on the HLS and depot.[^cite_note-153]

If Starship's second stage lands on a pad, a mobile hydraulic lift will move it to a transporter vehicle. If it lands on a [floating platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_spaceport_drone_ship), it will be transported by a [barge](4177.md) to a port and then transported by road. The recovered Starship will either be positioned on the launch mount for another launch or refurbished at a [SpaceX facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_facilities).[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137]<sup>: 22 </sup>

## Development

### Early design concepts (2012–2019)

Main article: [SpaceX Starship design history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_design_history)



SpaceX CEO [Elon Musk](909036.md) holding a model of BFR (Prior name of upper stage of Starship vehicle)

In November 2005,[^cite_note-tsr20051114-154] before SpaceX had launched its first rocket the [Falcon 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_1),[^cite_note-155] CEO [Elon Musk](909036.md) first mentioned a high-capacity rocket concept able to launch 100 t (220,000 lb) to [low Earth orbit](47568.md), dubbed the *BFR*.[^cite_note-tsr20051114-154] Later in 2012, Elon Musk first publicly announced plans to develop a rocket surpassing the capabilities of Space X's existing [Falcon 9](2647515.md).[^cite_note-fg201210152-156] SpaceX called it the *[Mars Colonial Transporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mars_Colonial_Transporter)*, as the rocket was to transport humans to Mars and back.[^cite_note-nsf20140307-100] In 2016, the descriptor was changed to *[Interplanetary Transport System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_Transport_System)*, as the rocket was planned to travel beyond Mars as well.[^cite_note-ars20160918-157] The conceptual design called for a [carbon fiber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_fiber) structure,[^cite_note-nsf20160927a-158] a mass in excess of 10,000 t (22,000,000 lb) when fully fueled, a payload of 300 t (660,000 lb) to low Earth orbit while being fully reusable.[^cite_note-nsf20160927a-158] By 2017, the concept was again re-dubbed the *[BFR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Falcon_Rocket)*.[^cite_note-musk20170929-159]

In December 2018, the structural material was changed from carbon composites[^cite_note-nsf20160927a-158] to stainless steel,[^cite_note-Foust-2018-160] marking the transition from early design concepts of the Starship.[^cite_note-Chang-2019b-150] Musk cited numerous reasons for the change of material; low cost and ease of manufacture, increased strength of stainless steel at [cryogenic temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryogenic_rocket_engine), as well as its ability to withstand high heat.[^cite_note-Chang-2019b-150] In 2019, SpaceX began to refer to the entire vehicle as Starship, with the second stage also being called [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)), and the booster [Super Heavy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_\(rocket_stage\)).[^cite_note-ars20190929-161] They also announced that Starship would use reusable heat-shield tiles similar to [those of the Space Shuttle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle_thermal_protection_system).[^cite_note-162] The second-stage design had also settled on six Raptor engines by 2019: three [optimized for sea-level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_engine_nozzle#Atmospheric_use) and three [optimized for vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_engine_nozzle#Vacuum_use).[^cite_note-Pappalardo-2019-163] In 2019, SpaceX announced a change to the second stage's design, reducing the number of aft flaps from three to two to reduce weight.[^cite_note-Foust-2019-164] In March 2020 SpaceX released a Starship Users Guide, in which they stated the payload of Starship to Low Earth Orbit (LEO) would be over 100 t (220,000 lb), with a payload to GTO of 21 t (46,000 lb).[^cite_note-SpaceX-users-guide-32]

### Low-altitude flight tests (2019–2021)

See also: [List of Starship upper stage flight tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_upper_stage_flight_tests)

#### *Starhopper* to SN6

Further information: [List of Starship vehicles § Hops (SN3–SN6)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Hops_\(SN3%E2%80%93SN6\))

Short steel rocket with its fins touching the ground

Starhopper under construction, March 2019

Crane hooking onto a steel vessel body

A crane lifting Starship SN5, August 2020



Static fire of SN4

The first tests started with the construction of the first prototype in 2018, *[Starhopper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starhopper)*, which performed several [static fires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Static_fire) and two successful low-altitude flights in 2019.[^cite_note-Harwood-2019-165] SpaceX began constructing the first full-size [Starship MK1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK1) and [MK2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK2) upper-stage prototypes before 2019, at the SpaceX facilities in [Boca Chica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boca_Chica_\(Texas\)), [Texas](29810.md), and [Cocoa, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa,_Florida), respectively.[^cite_note-Ryan-2019-166] Neither prototype flew: MK1 was destroyed in November 2019 during a pressure [stress test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stress_testing) and MK2's Florida facility was deconstructed throughout 2020.[^cite_note-167] Prototypes were built using [301 stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAE_steel_grades#300_Series%E2%80%94austenitic_chromium-nickel_alloys).[^cite_note-168] This was noted for its corrosion resistance and lower cost compared to carbon fiber but faced some challenges, particularly with interlaminar toughness at cryogenic temperatures.

SpaceX then began naming its new Starship upper-stage prototypes with the prefix "SN", short for "[serial number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serial_number)".[^cite_note-Berger-2020a-169] No prototypes between [SN1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN1) and [SN4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN4) flew either—SN1 and [SN3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN3) collapsed during pressure stress tests, and SN4 exploded after its fifth engine firing.[^cite_note-NSF12-170]

In June 2020, SpaceX started constructing a launch pad for orbital Starship flights.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The first flight-capable prototype, [SN5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN5), was [cylindrical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cylinder) as it had no flaps or nose cone: just one Raptor engine, fuel tanks, and a [mass simulator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boilerplate_\(spaceflight\)).[^cite_note-171] On August 5, 2020, SN5 performed a 150 m (500 ft) high flight and successfully landed on a nearby pad.[^cite_note-172] On September 3, 2020, the similar-looking [Starship SN6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN6) repeated the hop;[^cite_note-173] later that month, a [Raptor vacuum engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_vacuum_engine) underwent its first full duration firing at [McGregor, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGregor,_Texas).[^cite_note-174]

#### SN8 to SN15

Further information: [List of Starship vehicles § High-altitude test flights (SN8–SN15)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#High-altitude_test_flights_\(SN8%E2%80%93SN15\))



SN8 shortly after taking off, December 2020

Photograph of Elon Musk alongside the remains of Starship

SN8 remains after hard landing and subsequent explosion

Computer animation depicting a successful high-altitude flight test such as SN10 and SN15

[Starship SN8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN8) was the first full-sized upper-stage prototype, though it lacked a heat shield.[^cite_note-175] It underwent four preliminary static fire tests between October and November 2020.[^cite_note-NSF12-170] On December 9, 2020, SN8 flew, slowly turning off its three engines one by one, and reached an altitude of 12.5 km (7.8 mi). After SN8 dove back to the ground, its engines were hampered by low methane header tank pressure during the landing attempt, which led to a hard impact on the landing pad and subsequent explosion of the vehicle.[^cite_note-20201210cnn-tech-151] SN7 used [304L stainless steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAE_304_stainless_steel), which is less brittle and more weldable.[^cite_note-176] Later vehicles used a proprietary alloy, 30X, whose composition is proprietary that costs slightly over €3.6/kg.[^cite_note-177]

Because SpaceX had violated its [launch license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Launch_license) and ignored warnings of worsening [shock wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_wave) damage, the [Federal Aviation Administration](11186.md) investigated the incident for two months.[^cite_note-Roulette-2021-178] During the SN8 launch, SpaceX ignored FAA warnings that the flight profile posed a risk of explosion.[^cite_note-Roulette-2021-178] FAA space division chief [Wayne Monteith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_Monteith) said SpaceX's violation was "inconsistent with a strong safety culture", and criticized the company for proceeding with the launch "based on 'impressions' and 'assumptions,' rather than procedural checks and positive affirmations".[^cite_note-Roulette-2021-178]

On February 2, 2021, [Starship SN9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN9) launched to 10 km (6.2 mi) in a flight path similar to SN8. The prototype crashed upon landing because one engine did not ignite properly.[^cite_note-179] A month later, on March 3, [Starship SN10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN10) launched on the same flight path as SN9.[^cite_note-180] The vehicle landed hard and crushed its landing legs, leaning to one side.[^cite_note-Chang-2021-181] A fire was seen at the vehicle's base and it exploded less than ten minutes later,[^cite_note-FoustSurvives-182] potentially due to a propellant tank rupture.[^cite_note-Chang-2021-181] On March 30, [Starship SN11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN11) flew into thick fog along the same flight path.[^cite_note-Mack-2021a-183] The vehicle exploded during descent,[^cite_note-Mack-2021a-183] possibly due to excess propellant in a Raptor's methane turbopump.[^cite_note-Foust-2021-1-184]

In March 2021, the company disclosed a public construction plan for two [sub-orbital](455295.md) launch pads, two orbital launch pads, two landing pads, two test stands, and a large propellant tank farm.[^cite_note-ars-20210308-185] The company soon proposed developing the surrounding [Boca Chica Village, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boca_Chica_Village), into a [company town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_town) named [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase).[^cite_note-ars-20210308-185] Locals raised concerns about SpaceX's authority, power, and a potential threat for eviction through [eminent domain](144089.md).[^cite_note-186] In 2025, it was [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Starbase_Incorporation_election) as [Starbase, Texas](41620458.md).[^cite_note-bloomberg_starbase-187]

In early April, the orbital launch pad's fuel storage tanks began mounting.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] SN12 through SN14 were scrapped before completion; [SN15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_SN15) was selected to fly instead,[^cite_note-Mack-2021b-188] due to improved [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics), structure, and engines.[^cite_note-FoustSurvives-182] On May 5, 2021, SN15 launched, completed the same maneuvers as older prototypes, and landed safely.[^cite_note-Mack-2021b-188] SN15 had a fire in the engine area after landing but it was extinguished.[^cite_note-FoustSurvives-182] According to a later report by SpaceX, SN15 experienced several issues while landing, including the loss of tank pressure and an engine.[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023a-189]<sup>: 2 </sup>

### Integrated flight tests (2023–)

See also: [List of Starship launches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_launches)

In June 2022, the [Federal Aviation Administration](11186.md) determined that SpaceX must address more than 75 issues identified in the preliminary environmental assessment before flight tests could start.[^cite_note-Chang-2022-190]

#### First flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1)



Starship during the first flight attempt. Several engines failed on the first stage.

In July 2022, [Booster 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7) tested the liquid oxygen [turbopumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbopump) on all 33 Raptor engines, resulting in an explosion at the vehicle's base, which destroyed a pressure pipe and caused minor damage to the launchpad.[^cite_note-191] By the end of November, Ship 24 had performed 2 static test fires,[^cite_note-Kshatriya-2022-192]<sup>: 20 </sup> while Booster 7 had performed 6 static test fires[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-Kshatriya-2022-192]<sup>: 20 </sup> and finally on February 9, 2023, a static fire with 31 engines at 50% throttle.[^cite_note-194] In January 2023, the whole Starship stack underwent a full [wet dress rehearsal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_dress_rehearsal).[^cite_note-195]

After a launch attempt aborted on April 17, 2023,[^cite_note-196] [Booster 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7) and [Ship 24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_24) lifted off on April 20 at 13\:33 UTC in the first orbital flight test.[^cite_note-Jackie_Wattles_2023-197] Three engines were disabled during the launch sequence and several more failed during the flight.[^cite_note-Bergin-2023-198] The booster later lost [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring) control of the Raptor engines, which led to the rocket spinning out of control.[^cite_note-Bergin-2023-198] The vehicle reached a maximum altitude of 24 mi (39 km).[^cite_note-199] Approximately 3 minutes after lift-off the rocket's [autonomous flight termination system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_safety) was activated, though the vehicle tumbled for another 40 seconds before disintegrating.[^cite_note-200] The first flight test blasted large amounts of sand and soil in the air, reaching communities within a 10.7 km (6.6 mi) radius.[^cite_note-Leinfelder-2023-201] A brushfire on nearby state parkland also occurred, burning 3.5 acres (1.4 ha) of state parkland.[^cite_note-202]

#### Second flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2)



Starship during the second flight attempt

After the first test flight, SpaceX began work on the launch mount to repair the damage it sustained during the test and to prevent future issues. The foundation of the launch tower was reinforced and a water-powered [flame deflector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_deflector#SpaceX_Starship_launch_mount) was built under the launch mount.[^cite_note-203] [Ship 25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_25) and [Booster 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B9) were rolled to the suborbital and orbital launch sites in May to undergo multiple tests.[^cite_note-204]

On November 18, 2023, Booster 9 and Ship 25 lifted off the pad.[^cite_note-205] All 33 engines continued to function until staging, where the second stage separated by pushing itself away from the first stage using a [hot-staging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-staging) technique.[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023b-141] Following separation, the Super Heavy booster completed its flip maneuver and initiated the boostback burn, but then experienced multiple successive engine failures and exploded.[^cite_note-206] Blockage in a liquid oxygen filter caused one of the engines to fail in a way that resulted in the destruction of the booster, which occurred three and a half minutes into the flight at an altitude of \~90 km over the Gulf of Mexico.[^cite_note-ars-ift2-postmortem-207]

The second stage continued until it reached an altitude of \~149 kilometres (93 mi), after over eight minutes of flight; before engine cutoff, telemetry was lost on the second stage.[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023b-141] SpaceX said that a safe command based on flight performance data triggered the flight termination system and destroyed the second stage,[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023b-141] before achieving its planned orbit or attempting re-entry.[^cite_note-208] It appeared to re-enter a few hundred miles north of the [Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Islands), according to [NOAA](37876.md) weather radar data.[^cite_note-209]

#### Third flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3)

Video of Starship during the third flight test

Following the second flight test (which saw the loss of both stages), significant changes were implemented, including upgrading Starship's thrust vector control system to electric [thrust vector control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring) (TVC)[^cite_note-SpaceX-2024b-210] and measures to delay [liquid oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid_oxygen) (LOX)[^cite_note-SpaceX-2024b-210] venting until after Starship engine cutoff (SECO) has taken place.

Flight 3 launched from the [SpaceX Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase) facility along the [South Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Texas) coast around 8\:25 a.m. CDT on March 14, 2024, coincidentally the 22nd anniversary of the founding of SpaceX.[^cite_note-211] Like flight 2, all 33 engines on the booster ignited and stage separation was successful.[^cite_note-nsf-stream-212] B10 conducted a [boostback burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boostback_burn), however, the planned [landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_landing) in the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md) was not successful, as it exploded at 462 m (1,516 ft) above the surface.[^cite_note-SpaceX-2024a-143]

The Starship spacecraft itself, after reaching space and orbital velocity, conducted several tests after engine cutoff, including initiating a [propellant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propellant) transfer demo and payload dispenser test.[^cite_note-213] It attempted to [re-enter the atmosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry),[^cite_note-Berger-2024-214] and at an altitude of around 65 km (40 mi), all telemetry from Ship 28 stopped, indicating a loss of the vehicle.[^cite_note-ars-ift3-progress-215] This flight test demonstrated a cryogenic propellant transfer, by transferring propellant from the Ship's header tanks into its main tanks while in space, a technology which is required for Starship HLS to exit [Low Earth orbit](47568.md) (LEO). The result of this test was declared successful by NASA and SpaceX. Additional data analysis is occurring on the [fluid dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluid_dynamics) such as slosh and boil-off of the propellant.[^cite_note-abc-20240315-216]

#### Fourth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4)

The fourth flight test of the full Starship configuration launched on June 6, 2024, at 7\:50 a.m. CDT.[^cite_note-217] The goals for the test flight were for the Super Heavy booster to land on a 'virtual tower' in the ocean, and for the Ship to survive peak heating during atmospheric reentry.[^cite_note-Davenport-2024-218] The flight test was successful in both regards, with Super Heavy achieving a soft splashdown and Ship surviving atmospheric reentry and a controlled splashdown despite significant damage to the flight control surfaces.[^cite_note-219]

#### Fifth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5)



[Booster 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_12) is successfully caught by the launch tower during flight test 5.

In April 2024, Musk stated one of the goals was to attempt a booster tower landing based on successful booster performance in flight 4. Vehicle testing commenced in May 2024.[^cite_note-Flight_4-220] SpaceX claimed that B12 and S30 were ready to launch in early August, in advance of regulatory approval.[^cite_note-221] SpaceX flew S30 and B12 on October 13, 2024, with B12 returning to the launch site for a successful catch for the first time, and S30 successfully splashing down in the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-222]

#### Sixth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6)

Ship 31 completed a successful cryogenic test in July 2024 and a static fire in September.[^cite_note-223] Booster 13 completed similar tests in April and October.[^cite_note-224] Flight 6 was flown on November 19, 2024, with a water landing of the booster rather than a catch.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight-2024c-225] Flight 6 was the first to successfully conduct a Raptor engine relight in the vacuum of space, paving the way for [payload](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payload) deployments on future flights.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight-2024c-225] A [stuffed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_toy) toy [banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana) served as the zero-g indicator, becoming Starship's first payload, though it remained within the vehicle for the duration of the flight.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight-2024c-225] [Eric Berger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Berger_\(journalist\)) claimed that due to the success of the in-space relight, Starship would likely be "cleared to travel into orbit".[^cite_note-226]

#### Seventh flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7)

Ship 33 completed a successful cryogenic test in October 2024[^cite_note-227] and a static fire in late December.[^cite_note-228] Its counterpart, Booster 14, also underwent cryogenic testing in October.[^cite_note-229] Booster 14 rolled out to OLP-1 and conducted a successful spin prime test and static fire in early December.[^cite_note-230] Flight 7 was flown on January 16, 2025; the mission profile for flight test 7 was expected to be similar to the previous launch, targeting a splashdown in the Indian Ocean after attempting an in-space engine relight. Ship 33 was also expected to deploy ten Starlink "simulators," which were also expected to reenter over the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-231] Contact with Ship 33 was lost shortly before its engines were scheduled to shut down.[^cite_note-SS-232] Subsequently, Ship 33 was seen exploding as it flew over the [Turks and Caicos Islands](30217.md). The booster successfully returned to the launch site and was caught by the chopsticks on OLP-A.[^cite_note-SS-232] As a result of the explosion, numerous commercial airline flights were diverted or delayed.[^cite_note-233]

#### Eighth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_8)

On March 3, 2025, a launch attempt was aborted after multiple holds at *T*−40 seconds.[^cite_note-234] Shortly before the scheduled launch at 6\:45 p.m. CDT, an issue caused a hold for more than five minutes. The hold was briefly lifted, but a new hold was put in place due to issues connected to the Super Heavy booster.[^cite_note-235] SpaceX called for a cancellation of the launch and set March 6 for the launch.[^cite_note-236] The eighth flight test was later launched on March 6, 2025, at 23\:30 hours UTC. The Super Heavy booster was successfully caught by the launch tower. During Ship 34's initial burn, four of its six engines experienced premature shutdowns that resulted in a loss of attitude control followed by a total loss of telemetry. The vehicle's breakup was observed from [Florida](18933066.md), [Jamaica](15660.md), and the [Turks and Caicos Islands](30217.md).[^cite_note-237] According to SpaceX, communications with the spacecraft ended 9 minutes and 30 seconds after liftoff.[^cite_note-238] The flight was the second of the Block 2 Ship, and attempted to repeat the previous flight's profile.[^cite_note-SpaceX_Flight_8-239] Due to the breakup of the vehicles, the Federal Aviation Administration briefly issued ground stop orders for multiple Florida airports.[^cite_note-240]

#### Ninth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9)

Starship's [ninth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9) launched on May 27, 2025, with Booster 14, the program's first reused Super Heavy booster.[^cite_note-SL-241] The booster completed its ascent, executed a boost-back and entry burn and re-entered at a higher angle of attack than previous flights, but was lost before its planned splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico.[^cite_note-242] Ship 35 reached engine cutoff, yet a propellant leak caused loss of attitude control preventing reignition of a raptor engine and the payload bay door failed to open preventing deployment of the dummy starlink satellites; the vehicle broke up during re-entry.[^cite_note-SL-241]

#### Ship 36 explosion

On June 18, 2025, Ship 36, the Block 2 upper stage slated for the program's tenth flight test, exploded during a planned six-engine static-fire at SpaceX's Massey test stand near Starbase, Texas.[^cite_note-NSFShip36-243] Preliminary analysis suggested the failure of a composite-overwrapped pressure vessel (COPV) in the nose section, which ruptured and ignited methane and liquid-oxygen propellants.[^cite_note-SFN2025-244] No personnel were injured, but SpaceX paused further testing to inspect Block 2 pressurization hardware and repair the damaged stand.[^cite_note-245]

#### Tenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10)

Starship's [tenth flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10) was scheduled for August 24, 2025, but was scrubbed shortly before liftoff.[^cite_note-246] After a second aborted attempt on August 25, Flight 10 successfully launched on August 26. The booster completed the ascent, boostback, and landing burns despite losing an engine on ascent, and the ship deployed its payload, eight Starlink simulators. It then completed a relight of a single engine before reentry and successfully splashed down in the Indian Ocean within a few meters of its target.[^cite_note-247]

#### Eleventh flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11)

Starship's [eleventh flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11) launched on October 13, 2025, mostly repeating the mission profile of the tenth flight, though with a different arrangement of intentionally missing tiles.[^cite_note-launchstream20251013-248] The second booster to be reused, Booster 15-2 completed its ascent, boostback, and landing burns with a single engine failure during boostback. The failed engine later relit for the landing burn. After SECO, Ship 38 began performing its experiments, deploying eight Starlink simulators and relighting one of its engines before reentering Earth's atmosphere.[^cite_note-launchstream20251013-248] During reentry, the ship conducted multiple banking maneuvers, including a simulated trajectory which will be used in future missions for ship catch. At approximately T+1\:05\:54, at an altitude of 1 km and descent speed of 338 km/h, the ship relit its three sea-level engines, flipped vertical, and softly splashed down before tipping over and exploding.[^cite_note-launchstream20251013-248] This was the final launch of the Block 2 configuration of Starship and Super Heavy.

#### Twelfth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12)

Starship's twelfth Starship test launch was on May 22, 2026, using Ship 39 and Booster 19. This flight was the first test of Version 3 of Starship.[^cite_note-249] It conducted a successful ascent burn with 33 engines, but later one Raptor engine failed.[^cite_note-\:0-63] Just before hot staging, the booster purposely went down to five engines. After stage separation, Ship 39 nominally lit all six engines.[^cite_note-250] Booster 19 went into an off-nominal flip. SpaceX tried to light all 33 engines for a few seconds of flight, but only 29 lit. Shortly after, an explosion in engine number E6 knocked out other Raptor engines near it. Only 10 engines were spared and the booster ended its boostback burn prematurely. The booster lost control and broke apart over the Gulf of Mexico.[^cite_note-251]

#### Thirteenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 13](78384679.md)

Aiming to complete the same goals as flight 12, except with functional Starlink V3 satellites instead of [mass simulators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_simulator), Starship's [thirteenth flight test](78384679.md) was initially scheduled for July 16, 2026, but scrubbed shortly after main engine ignition due to unexpected ice buildup in several Raptor 3 engine turbopumps.[^cite_note-252] After a second scrub on July 23 due to weather, Flight 13 successfully launched on July 24 with booster 20 and ship 40. Booster 20 successfully conducted ascent and boostback with all 33 engines, before attempting a soft splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico. Booster 20 attempted ignition on all 13 center engines, but only 10 lit, with 2 additional engines failing shortly after. Booster then dropped to 5 engines as planned, but failed to bleed off enough speed before being destroyed in a hard splashdown. Ship 40 nominally completed suborbital insertion and deployed 20 Starlink V3 satellites before successfully conducting an in-vacuum relight of one sea-level Raptor engine. Ship continued through reentry and achieved soft splashdown in the Indian Ocean, becoming the first Starship to remain intact after toppling over post-splashdown.[^cite_note-253]

#### Fourteenth flight test

Main article: [Starship flight test 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_14)

Marking a significant expansion of Starship's objectives, on September 28, 2026, flight 14 entered [Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_orbit) and completed the first operational [Starlink](45111627.md) deployment.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255] The mission deployed 26 operational Starlink V3 satellites.[^cite_note-256] During Ship 41's ascent, one of its three [Raptor Vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_Vacuum) engines shut down prematurely.[^cite_note-SPOOrbit-257] SpaceX initially announced that the spacecraft would not commit to orbit, but reversed that decision after evaluating its condition.[^cite_note-ReutersOrbit-258] Approximately 25 minutes after liftoff, Ship 41 performed a short orbital-insertion burn using a single Raptor engine and entered orbit.[^cite_note-ReutersOrbit-258][^cite_note-SPOOrbit-257] SpaceX shortened the flight rather than continuing the original multi-orbit mission profile.[^cite_note-APOrbit-259][^cite_note-ReutersOrbit2-260]

## Cost and funding

SpaceX develops the Starship primarily with [private funding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_capital).[^cite_note-ars20190929-161] SpaceX Chief Financial Officer [Bret Johnsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bret_Johnsen) disclosed in court that SpaceX has invested more than \$3 billion into the [Starbase facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase) and Starship systems from July 2014 to May 2023.[^cite_note-Kolodny-2023-261] Elon Musk stated in April 2023 that SpaceX expected to spend about \$2 billion on Starship development in 2023.[^cite_note-Maidenberg-2023-262] In a 2024 response to a lawsuit, SpaceX stated that the cost of the Starship program was approximately \$4 million per day,[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight-2024a-263]<sup>: 25–26 </sup> adding that any day of delay to the Starship program represented a loss of \$100,000.[^cite_note-NASASpaceflight-2024a-263]<sup>: 25–26 </sup>

Musk has theorized that a Starship orbital launch might eventually cost SpaceX only \$1 million to launch.[^cite_note-264] [Eurospace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurospace)'s director of research Pierre Lionnet stated in 2022 that Starship's launch price to customers would likely be higher because of the rocket's development cost.[^cite_note-Scoles-2022-265] In an update on their website in late October 2025,[^cite_note-266] SpaceX stated regarding Starship that "SpaceX is self-funding representing over 90% of system costs" \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*].[^cite_note-267]

As part of the development of the [Human Landing System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Landing_System) for the [Artemis program](60758751.md), SpaceX was awarded in April 2021 a \$2.89 billion fixed-price contract from NASA to develop the [Starship lunar lander](64020556.md) for [Artemis III](50493439.md).[^cite_note-Brown-2021-268] [Blue Origin](887418.md), a bidding competitor to SpaceX, disputed the decision and [began a legal case against NASA and SpaceX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Origin_v._United_States_&_Space_Exploration_Technologies_Corp.) in August 2021, causing NASA to suspend the contract for three months until the case was dismissed in the [Court of Federal Claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Federal_Claims).[^cite_note-Pruitt-Young-2021-269] Two years later Blue Origin was awarded a \$3.4 billion fixed-price contract for [its lunar lander](54252087.md).[^cite_note-O'Shea-2023-270]

In 2022, NASA awarded SpaceX a \$1.15 billion fixed-price contract for a second lunar lander for [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV).[^cite_note-news.yahoo.com-271] The same year, SpaceX was awarded a \$102 million five-year contract to develop the [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo) program for the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force).[^cite_note-Erwin-2022-272]
