### Past launches

### 2023

| Flight No. | Date and time ([UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time)) | Version, booster[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Version, ship[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Launch site | Payload | Payload mass | Orbit | Customer | Launch outcome | Booster landing | Ship landing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | April 20, 2023 13\:33\:09[^cite_note-274] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B7) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S24](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_24) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | —N/a | —N/a | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit)[^cite_note-jsp819-275] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Failure | Precluded | Precluded |
| [Flight 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_1) was the first with a ship integrated with the [Super Heavy booster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Super_Heavy), the booster was planned to make a powered splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico, and the ship would enter a [transatmospheric Earth orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) before reentering and impacting the Pacific Ocean north of Hawaii. Several engines were shut down before and during flight. The vehicle eventually entered an uncontrolled spin before stage separation due to loss of [thrust vector control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vector_control). The [flight termination system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_termination_system) activated but the vehicle remained intact for 40 more seconds.[^cite_note-276] SpaceX declared this flight a success, as their primary goal was to only clear the pad.[^cite_note-277] The launch resulted in extensive damage to the orbital launch mount and the infrastructures around it, including the propellant tank farm.[^cite_note-278] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 2 | November 18, 2023 13\:02\:50[^cite_note-279] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_B9) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_25) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | —N/a | —N/a | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit)[^cite_note-jsp826-280] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Failure | Failure (gulf) | Precluded |
| [Flight 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2) had a test flight profile similar to the first flight, with the addition of a new hot-staging technique and the introduction of a water deluge system as part of the ground support equipment at the launch pad. During the first stage ascent, all 33 engines fired to full duration. Starship and Super Heavy successfully accomplished a [hot-staging separation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_staging). After initiating a flip maneuver and initiating boostback burn, the booster suffered engine shutdowns and was lost.[^cite_note-SpaceX_2024-02-26-281] The upper stage ascended nominally[^cite_note-282] until the automated flight termination system destroyed it due to loss of communications from a fire.[^cite_note-SpaceX_2024-02-26-281] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### 2024

| Flight No. | Date and time ([UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time)) | Version, booster[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Version, ship[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Launch site | Payload | Payload mass | Orbit | Customer | Launch outcome | Booster landing | Ship landing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3 | March 14, 2024 13\:25\:00[^cite_note-283] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_10) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_28) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](455295.md)[^cite_note-JSR-284] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Failure (gulf) | Failure (ocean) |
| [Flight 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_3) included a full-duration burn of the second-stage engines, an internal propellant-transfer demonstration, and a test of the Starlink dispenser door. The planned in-space engine relight of the spacecraft and its hard splashdown into the Indian Ocean did not occur.[^cite_note-285] The booster successfully propelled the spacecraft to staging, with 13 engines ignited for a boostback burn, though six engines shut down prematurely. During the landing burn ignition, only three engines ignited, and the booster was lost around 400 meters above the ocean.[^cite_note-SpaceX_3rd_test-286] At reentry, Ship had an uncontrolled roll and lost telemetry, leading SpaceX to conclude the ship was lost during reentry. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 4 | June 6, 2024 12\:50\:00[^cite_note-287] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_11) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S29](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_29) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](455295.md)[^cite_note-jsr834-288] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_4) flew a similar trajectory to Flight 3, with the addition of a ship landing burn and soft splashdown. One Raptor engine was lost shortly after liftoff, but the booster performed in accordance to its flight profile and conducted a controlled splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico[^cite_note-289] on a "virtual tower", in preparation for a catch by the launch tower during Flight 5.[^cite_note-290] The spacecraft performed a successful reentry despite severe forward flap damage and conducted a controlled splashdown in the Indian Ocean,[^cite_note-291] within the target region but 6 kilometers from the center.[^cite_note-292] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 5 | October 13, 2024 12\:25\:00[^cite_note-293] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_12) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_30) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | —N/a | —N/a | [Suborbital](455295.md)[^cite_note-sn20241009-294] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Success (OLP-1) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_5) was the first to achieve booster recovery and complete a flight without engine failures. After stage separation, the booster [returned to the launch site](1306157.md#Return_to_launch_site) and was caught by the launch tower arms despite damage to a chine during descent. Following a coast phase, Ship 30 reentered the atmosphere, performed reentry despite forward flap damage, and executed a landing burn, splashing down at its target in the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-Weber_2024-295] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 6 | November 19, 2024 22\:00\:00[^cite_note-space20241119-296] | [Block 1](#Block_1) [B13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_13) | [Block 1](#Block_1) [S31](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_31) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | [Plush banana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_toy) | Unknown | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit)[^cite_note-297] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | <sup>Controlled (gulf)</sup><sub>Abort (OLP‑1)</sub> | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_6) was the second attempt at booster recovery and the final use of a Block 1 upper stage. Heat shield tiles were removed from key areas of Ship 31, which also lacked the ablative backup layer from Flight 5. Following stage separation, the booster was diverted to the ocean near the launch site due to damage to the catch tower during liftoff. The ship completed an in-space engine relight test and re-entered, splashing down in the Indian Ocean during daylight—a first for Starship. Despite a reduced heat shield and steeper re-entry trajectory, Ship 31 sustained minimal flap damage. The flight also carried Starship's first payload, a toy stuffed banana serving as the zero-gravity indicator, which remained onboard throughout the mission.[^cite_note-nsf20241118-298] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### 2025

In a talk in November 2024, Starbase General Manager [Kathy Lueders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathy_Lueders) announced that SpaceX hoped to be able to catch a [Starship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starship_\(spacecraft\)) upper stage sometime in the next 6 months and have as many as 25 launches in 2025.[^cite_note-299] This did not occur. Starship was launched five times in 2025.

| Flight No. | Date and time ([UTC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Universal_Time)) | Version, booster[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Version, ship[^cite_note-vehicle-273] | Launch site | Payload | Payload mass | Orbit | Customer | Launch outcome | Booster landing | Ship landing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 7 | January 16, 2025 22\:37\:00[^cite_note-300] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [B14‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_14)[^cite_note-301] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [S33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_33)[^cite_note-McCrea_2024-07-31-302] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | 10 [Starlink](45111627.md) simulator satellites[^cite_note-303] | \~20,000 kg (44,000 lb)[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit)[^cite_note-jsp841-306] | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Failure | Success (OLP-1) | Precluded |
| [Flight 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_7) was to follow a trajectory similar to the previous mission, with a planned splashdown in the Indian Ocean approximately one hour post-launch.[^cite_note-McCrea_2024-11-27-307] It marked the inaugural flight of a Block 2 Ship, featuring structural, avionics, and other upgrades.[^cite_note-McCrea_2024-07-31-302] The mission also aimed to test the deployment system for 10 [Starlink](45111627.md) mass simulator satellites. During the Ship's initial burn, its engines experienced premature shutdowns due to a propellant leak larger than the Ship's systems could handle, followed by a total loss of telemetry. SpaceX stated the autonomous flight safety system destroyed the Ship about three minutes after loss of telemetry.[^cite_note-NY-308] The booster successfully returned to the launch site, where it was caught by the launch tower arms on OLP-1 without noticeable damage to the chines.[^cite_note-Clark_2025-309] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 8 | March 6, 2025 23\:31\:02[^cite_note-SF8-310] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [B15‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_15)[^cite_note-McCrea_2024-11-27-307] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [S34](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_34)[^cite_note-Bergin_2024-311] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | 4 [Starlink](45111627.md) simulator satellites[^cite_note-SF8-310] | \~8,000 kg (18,000 lb)[^cite_note-SF8-310] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Failure | Success (OLP-1) | Precluded |
| [Flight 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_8) was to follow a trajectory similar to the previous mission, with a planned splashdown in the Indian Ocean. During the Ship's initial burn, several engines shut down and the Ship lost control and later telemetry.[^cite_note-312] The booster was successfully commanded to return to the launch site despite having two engines fail to relight for its boostback burn. One of the failed engines managed to reignite for the catch, which was successful.[^cite_note-SF8-310] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 9 | May 27, 2025 23\:36\:28[^cite_note-spacexflight9-313] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [B14-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_14)[^cite_note-314] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [S35](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_35)[^cite_note-315] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | 8 [Starlink](45111627.md) simulator satellites[^cite_note-spacexflight9-313] | \~16,000 kg (35,000 lb)[^cite_note-spacexflight9-313] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Failure | Failure (gulf) | Failure (ocean) |
| [Flight 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_9) was the first to reuse a Super Heavy booster, along with 29 engines having been used on a flight, which completed ascent and boostback into a high angle of attack but was lost before splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico.[^cite_note-F9-316] The ship reached engine cutoff but failed to deploy its payload of eight [Starlink](45111627.md) simulator satellites[^cite_note-spacexflight9-313] and experienced a fuel leak, resulting in a loss of control. The ship was [passivated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passivation_\(spacecraft\)) before reentry and broke up over the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-F9-316] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 10 | August 26, 2025 23\:30\:00[^cite_note-FT-317] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [B16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B16)[^cite_note-318] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [S37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Block_2_launches_\(S33-S38\))[^cite_note-319] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | 8 [Starlink](45111627.md) simulator satellites[^cite_note-FT-317] | \~16,000 kg (35,000 lb)[^cite_note-FT-317] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_10) was delayed by around two months[^cite_note-320] because the ship originally designated for the flight was lost during testing.[^cite_note-321] The booster ignited all thirty-three engines, though it lost one during the ascent burn. It would continue to complete its mission, splashing down in the Gulf of Mexico after simulating an engine out.[^cite_note-TA-322] The ship reached the desired trajectory and deployed all eight of its Starlink simulators.[^cite_note-TA-322] It then relit a single Raptor engine in space, followed by atmospheric entry.[^cite_note-TA-322] During descent through the atmosphere, there was substantial damage to the engine section.[^cite_note-TA-322] Despite this, S37 was able to softly splash down within three meters of its target site in the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-TA-322] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 11 | October 13, 2025 23\:23\:00[^cite_note-\:5-323] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [B15‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_15)[^cite_note-TB-324] | [Block 2](#Block_2) [S38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Block_2_launches_\(S33-S38\))[^cite_note-TB-324] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_1) | 8 Starlink simulator satellites[^cite_note-\:1-325] | \~16,000 kg (35,000 lb)[^cite_note-\:1-325] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_11) was the last flight of Block 2 vehicles,[^cite_note-FT-317] as well as the last flight from Pad-1 before its retrofit.[^cite_note-326] It flew a similar profile to the previous two flights,[^cite_note-\:3-327] with twenty-four engines flying for a second time on Booster 15.[^cite_note-328] The booster performed nominally during its flight, with the only anomaly being the loss of a Raptor engine on the boostback burn, though it would later reignite on the landing burn.[^cite_note-\:5-323] The ship, like on the previous flight, made it to SECO before deploying its eight Starlink simulators.[^cite_note-\:5-323] Following this, a single Raptor engine was lit in space, with the ship reentering shortly afterwards.[^cite_note-\:5-323] Unlike on Flight 10, the ship was mostly undamaged from heat on reentry, despite the intentional removal of several tiles.[^cite_note-\:5-323] S38 landed on target in the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-\:5-323] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 12 | May 22, 2026 22\:30\:00[^cite_note-329] | [Block 3](#Block_3) [B19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B19)[^cite_note-330] | [Block 3](#Block_3) [S39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Ship_39)[^cite_note-Weber_2025-331] | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_2)[^cite_note-Bergin_2025-332] | 20 Starlink simulator satellites and 2 modified Starlink V3[^cite_note-\:0-63] | \~37,500 kg (82,700 lb)[^cite_note-\:6-333] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Failure (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_12) was originally intended to feature Booster 18 and Ship 39; however, Booster 18 was scrapped after sustaining severe damage during testing.[^cite_note-334] This mission marked the debut of the "Block 3" vehicle iteration and the inaugural use of Starbase’s second launch pad.[^cite_note-\:7-335] It aimed for the controlled splashdown of both the Booster and the Ship.[^cite_note-\:7-335] The mission followed a flight profile similar to previous tests, with the ship targeting a trajectory just shy of orbit.[^cite_note-336] During ascent, the booster performed nominally despite losing a single outboard Raptor engine.[^cite_note-337] However, following hot-staging, Booster 19 only successfully relit 20 of the 33 engines intended for the boostback burn, the majority of which failed during startup.[^cite_note-\:7-335] The burn was then aborted after only a few seconds.[^cite_note-\:7-335] During the landing sequence, only one engine relit, leading the vehicle to impact the water at high speed.[^cite_note-\:7-335] Ship 39 suffered the loss of one Raptor Vacuum engine during its ascent, but the vehicle's engine-out capability allowed it to successfully reach its scheduled engine cutoff (SECO).[^cite_note-\:7-335][^cite_note-v3spacenews-338] While the ship successfully deployed 20 satellite mass simulators and two modified functional Starlink satellites, the planned in-space Raptor relight was skipped.[^cite_note-339] The two functional satellites were used to test upcoming Starlink V3 hardware, capture and relay footage of the Starship in space, and inspect its heat shield.[^cite_note-v3spacenews-338][^cite_note-340] Finally, the ship completed a nominal reentry and a successful landing flip using two engines as planned.[^cite_note-\:7-335] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 13 | July 24, 2026 22\:51\:00[^cite_note-\:8-341] | [Block 3](#Block_3) [B20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B20) | [Block 3](#Block_3) [S40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Ship_40) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_2) | 20 Starlink V3[^cite_note-\:8-341] | \~34,000 kg (75,000 lb)[^cite_note-flt12extrapolation-342][^cite_note-\:8-341][^cite_note-\:6-333] | [Transatmospheric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatmospheric_orbit) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Failure (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 13](78384679.md) followed a flight profile similar to previous tests. Liftoff, hot staging and boostback were nominal.[^cite_note-343] During the attempted landing burn, Booster 20 only lit 10 of the planned 13 engines on the landing burn and after losing five more, impacted the water at high speed. Flight 13 featured the first deployment of Starlink V3 satellites, which followed the trajectory of the ship and, after testing, burned up in the atmosphere as planned. After its simulated landing over the Indian Ocean, the ship splashed down and tipped over as planned. For the first time, the ship survived this, allowing SpaceX to get more images of the heat shield tiles, flaps and engine bay afterwards.[^cite_note-344] |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| 14 | September 28, 2026 12\:48[^cite_note-NPR_Flight_14-345] | [Block 3](#Block_3) [B21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Super_Heavy_boosters#B21) | [Block 3](#Block_3) [S41](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starship_vehicles#Ship_41) | [Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase), [OLP‑2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_Launch_Pad_2) | [Starlink](45111627.md): [Group 31-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Starlink_and_Starshield_launches#G15-25)[^cite_note-Reuters_Flight_14-346] | \~44,000 kg (97,000 lb)[^cite_note-flt12extrapolation-342][^cite_note-\:8-341][^cite_note-\:6-333] | [LEO](47568.md) | [SpaceX](832774.md) | Success | Controlled (gulf) | Controlled (ocean) |
| [Flight 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_14) was Starship's first orbital mission and its first deployment of an operational payload. Super Heavy Booster 21 completed ascent, hot-staging and a boostback before making a controlled splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico; several booster engines shut down during descent, but the landing remained controlled.[^cite_note-Reuters_Flight_14-346] During Ship 41's ascent, one of its six Raptor engines shut down early, initially prompting flight controllers to announce that the vehicle would not commit to orbit. After engineers confirmed sufficient system redundancy, SpaceX proceeded with the orbital-insertion burn, and Ship 41 reached an orbit at roughly 275 km (171 mi) altitude.[^cite_note-Reuters_Flight_14-346][^cite_note-Gizmodo_Flight_14-347] The ship then deployed all 26 operational Starlink V3 satellites, which entered operation nominally.[^cite_note-Reuters_Flight_14-346] SpaceX used the mission's first post-deployment health-check opportunity to return early rather than continue the planned six-orbit, nearly ten-hour coast. Ship 41 reentered and made a controlled Pacific Ocean splashdown at about 15\:57 UTC, roughly three hours after launch.[^cite_note-Gizmodo_Flight_14-347] The abbreviated flight therefore demonstrated orbital insertion, operational payload deployment, deorbit and controlled reentry on Starship's first mission beyond the suborbital test regime. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

Future launches are listed chronologically when firm plans are in place. Launches are expected to take place "no earlier than" (NET) the listed date.

| [Flight 15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_15) is provisionally targeted for no earlier than October 19, 2026.[^cite_note-CADENA_Flight_15_16-348] The flight is expected to use Booster 22 and Ship 42.[^cite_note-Flight_15_hardware-349] Before Flight 14, Elon Musk stated that it would be the final mission before an attempted catch of Starship's second stage and said a successful Flight 14 would clear the way for a ship-catch attempt on Flight 15. Musk estimated the probability of a successful first catch at "at least 50 or 60 percent".[^cite_note-Musk_Flight_15_catch-350] Flight 14 subsequently reached orbit, deployed all 26 Starlink V3 satellites and completed a controlled Pacific splashdown, although SpaceX shortened the originally planned six-orbit mission after an upper-stage engine shutdown.[^cite_note-Reuters_Flight_14-346][^cite_note-Gizmodo_Flight_14-347] As of September 28, SpaceX had not publicly reconfirmed whether Flight 15 would retain the ship-catch attempt. Ship 42 had already undergone multi-day catch-related testing at OLP-2 on September 11–12, including being lifted and supported by the launch tower's chopstick arms in a simulated catch position and tests involving the ship quick-disconnect arm.[^cite_note-S42_catch_testing-351] |  |  |  |  |  |  |

## Potential missions

### Starlink

SpaceX plans to use Starship to launch the second generation of satellites for SpaceX's [Starlink](45111627.md) system, which as of 2021 delivered high-speed internet to over 70 countries.[^cite_note-Sheetz-2021b-392] An analyst at financial services company [Morgan Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Stanley) stated development of Starship and Starlink are intertwined, with Starship's planned launch capacity enabling cheaper Starlink launches, and Starlink's profits financing Starship's development costs.[^cite_note-393] In deficit from its inception until the end of 2022,[^cite_note-394] Starlink was first reported to be cash flow positive in the first quarter of 2023,[^cite_note-395] though Elon Musk said that Starlink had only reached "break-even cashflow" in 2023.[^cite_note-396] In December 2023, the [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FCC) issued a final denial of a \$885 million Starlink [RDOF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Service_Fund#Connect_America_Fund) subsidy because of Starlink's "continuing inability to successfully launch on the Starship rocket".[^cite_note-fccdenial-397]

### Artemis Program

See caption and article

[Artemis III](50493439.md) launch profile of a human landing on the Moon, involving Starship HLS, Starship tanker variants, and [Orion spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_\(spacecraft\))&#x20;

[Starship HLS](64020556.md) was initially chosen by NASA as the sole lunar [Human Landing System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Landing_System) for the planned [Artemis III](50493439.md) and [Artemis IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis_IV) crewed missions, as part of the [Artemis program](60758751.md).[^cite_note-Burghardt-2021-398] Starship HLS is to be launched into a low Earth orbit, and refueled by multiple Starship tanker spacecraft.[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup> Once fueled, it would perform a trans lunar injection burn and enter a [near-rectilinear halo orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-rectilinear_halo_orbit)[^cite_note-Gerstenmaier-2023-400] around the Moon, with a [perilune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perilune) of 1,500 km (930 mi) occurring over the north pole and an [apolune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apolune) of 70,000 km (43,000 mi) occurring over the south pole.[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup> The [Orion spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_\(spacecraft\)) would then dock with Starship HLS and two of its four crew would transfer into Starship HLS.[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup> Starship HLS would then use its engines to make a powered descent and land near the [lunar south pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_south_pole).[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup> After the crew performs the surface portion of its mission, the HLS would ascend with the crew.[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup> The crew would then transfer into the Orion spacecraft and return to Earth.[^cite_note-NASA_IG-2021-399]<sup>: 4, 5 </sup>

### Astronomy

Astronomers have called to consider Starship's larger mass to orbit and wider cargo bay for proposed space telescopes such as [LUVOIR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Ultraviolet_Optical_Infrared_Surveyor), and to develop larger telescopes to take advantage of these capabilities.[^cite_note-Elvis-2023-401] Starship's 9 m (30 ft) fairing width could hold an 8 m (26 ft) wide space telescope mirror in a single piece,[^cite_note-Clark-2023-402] alleviating the need for complex unfolding such as that of the [JWST](434221.md)'s 6.5 m (21 ft) mirror, which added cost and delays.[^cite_note-Elvis-2023-401]

Regarding larger mass to orbit limits, the [Ariane 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariane_5) imposed a \~6,500 kg limit on the telescope's weight.[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] Starship's low launch cost could also allow probes to use heavier, more common, less expensive materials, such as glass instead of [beryllium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryllium) for large telescope mirrors.[^cite_note-Elvis-2023-401] With a 5 t (11,000 lb) mirror built using similar methods to the [Hubble Space Telescope](40203.md)'s mirror, the JWST would represent only 10% of the mass deliverable by a (refueled) Starship to the Sun–Earth [L2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point) point, and therefore minimizing the weight of the telescope would not have been a dominant design consideration.[^cite_note-Elvis-2023-401]

The [National Academies of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Sciences)'s [2020 survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy_and_Astrophysics_Decadal_Survey) recommended the [Habitable Worlds Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitable_Worlds_Observatory) (HWO); the space observatory, requiring a super heavy lift launch vehicle, will search for [signs of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosignature) on [exoplanets](9763.md).[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] The HWO's team hopes for the success of big launchers due to their critical importance to the HWO's mission.[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] Lee Feinberg, NASA HWO lead architect[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] and JWST manager,[^cite_note-404] stays in communication with SpaceX to track Starship's progress and has visited them in 2024 for that purpose.[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] The NASA Habitable Worlds Observatory will have a 6–8 meter mirror for now, but its design should be flexible to leverage launchers with potentially double the mass and volume by the time it launches in the 2040s.[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] Former NASA [JPL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_Propulsion_Laboratory) architect Casey Handmer believes the HWO to be far too conservative compared to what is possible with Starship.[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403] Handmer argues that Starship enables telescopes to scale up to the point of surface-level [exoplanet imaging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_directly_imaged_exoplanets), perhaps big enough to detect seasonal [migration patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_patterns).[^cite_note-Kuhr_2024-403]

### Rocket cargo

Main article: [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo)

In January 2022, SpaceX was awarded a \$102 million five-year contract to develop the [Rocket Cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket_Cargo) program for the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force).[^cite_note-Erwin-2022-272] The five-year contract is intended to "determine exactly what a rocket can achieve when used for cargo transport",[^cite_note-AeroTime-2022-405] and will see the [Air Force Research Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Research_Laboratory) collect data during commercial launches of Starship.[^cite_note-AeroTime-2022-405] The contract includes an eventual demonstration mission with the launch and landing of a cargo-laden Starship in a point-to-point flight.

The [Department of Defense](7279897.md) has planned a test with Starship as part of its program to demonstrate the ability to rapidly deploy up to 100 tons of cargo and supplies, a capability it calls point-to-point delivery (P2PD). The test was envisioned to take place in FY25 or FY26.[^cite_note-406]

### Mars Sample Return

In 2024, the [NASA-ESA Mars Sample Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA-ESA_Mars_Sample_Return) project, one of NASA's highest priority flagship projects, suffered a setback when an independent review board assessing the project's feasibility concluded that the project could not be completed under its mission profile. In April 2024, the [Administrator of NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrator_of_NASA) then announced that a new mission profile was needed for the project and that NASA would turn to industry for proposals, with responses due in fall 2024, and a high emphasis on lower total cost and lower risk.[^cite_note-407] Starship was proposed by some space journalists as a leading candidate to serve as a central component of the new mission profile architecture.[^cite_note-408]

### Transportation

SpaceX has proposed using Starship for [point-to-point flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceflight#Point-to-point) (called "Earth to Earth" flights by SpaceX), traveling anywhere on Earth in under an hour.[^cite_note-409] Musk stated that SpaceX would complete hundreds of cargo flights before launching with human passengers.[^cite_note-cnbc20200901-410]

### Space colonization

Further information: [SpaceX Mars colonization program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program)

According to SpaceX, the design of Starship is driven by its requirement to be able to land crews on Mars,[^cite_note-411]<sup>: 120 </sup> though SpaceX has not published technical plans or designs about Starship's [life support systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life-support_system), [radiation protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiation_protection), docking system, or in-orbit refueling system for Mars.[^cite_note-Grush-2019-412] The spacecraft would be launched to low Earth orbit and refueled in orbit before heading to Mars.[^cite_note-Pearson-2019-413] After landing on Mars, the [Sabatier reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabatier_reaction) could be used to synthesize liquid methane and liquid oxygen, Starship's fuel, in a [power-to-gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power-to-gas) plant.[^cite_note-414] The plant's [raw resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_situ_resource_utilization) would be [Martian water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_on_Mars) and [Martian carbon dioxide](4548379.md#Carbon_dioxide).[^cite_note-Sommerlad-2021-119] On Earth, similar technologies could be used to make [carbon-neutral propellant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon-neutral_fuel) for the rocket.[^cite_note-415] To date, there has been one proof of concept experiment ([MOXIE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOXIE)) demonstrating the extraction of oxygen from Martian carbon dioxide, with [George Dvorsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Dvorsky) writing for [Gizmodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gizmodo) commenting that we are not "remotely close" to turning this "into something practical".[^cite_note-Gizmodo-2022-416]

SpaceX and Musk have stated [their goal of colonizing Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Mars_colonization_program) to ensure the [long-term survival of humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_and_survival),[^cite_note-NYT-20160927-417] with an ambition of having sent one million people to Mars by 2050.[^cite_note-418] In March 2022, he estimated that the first crewed Mars landing could occur in 2029.[^cite_note-Torchinsky-2022-419] This timeline has been criticized as unrealistic by Kevin Olsen, a physicist at the [University of Oxford](31797.md), England, who has said that "colony needs to become a factory" to produce air, fuel and water as it is "fundamentally impossible to create a completely closed environment in space", and that the technology to do so is "far, far behind the technology of space flight and habitation construction".[^cite_note-Gizmodo-2022-416] Serkan Saydam, a mining engineering professor from the [University of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_New_South_Wales), Australia, stated that humanity currently lacks the necessary technology to establish a Martian colony, and will likely lack the capacity to establish a Martian city with one million people by 2050.[^cite_note-Gizmodo-2022-416]

### Other missions

A future payload is the [Superbird-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superbird-9) communication satellite, which was Starship's first contract for externally made commercial satellites.[^cite_note-420] Another planned payload is the [Starlab space station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starlab_\(space_station\)), which Starship expects to launch in a single piece.[^cite_note-spacecom-20240131-421]

In the future, the spacecraft's crewed version could be used for [space tourism](67896.md)—for example, for the third flight of the [Polaris program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polaris_program).[^cite_note-422]

Research conducted by [Project Lyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Lyra) determined that with refueling in LEO, a Starship could send a spacecraft to [ʻOumuamua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBOumuamua) on a journey taking 20 years.[^cite_note-Hibberd_2023-423] A gravity assist would be required at Jupiter.[^cite_note-Hibberd_2023-423]

## Facilities

Further information: [SpaceX Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase) and [SpaceX facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_facilities)

### Testing and manufacturing

Various spacecraft constructed inside bays

[Ship 27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_27), [Ship 26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_Ship_26) and [Booster 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Heavy_Booster_10) forward section under construction in Starbase build site, March 2023

[Starbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase) consists of a manufacturing facility and launch site,[^cite_note-424] and is located at Boca Chica, Texas. Both facilities operate 24 hours a day.[^cite_note-Berger-2020b-31] A maximum of 450 full-time employees may be onsite.[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137]<sup>: 28 </sup> The site consists of two launch sites, one payload processing facility, one seven-acre (2.8 ha) [solar farm](34779206.md), and other facilities.[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137]<sup>: 34–36 </sup>

Raptor engines are tested at the [Rocket Development facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_McGregor) in McGregor, Texas. The facility has two main test stands: one horizontal stand for both engine types and one vertical stand for sea-level-optimized rocket engines.[^cite_note-Davenport-2021-425] In the future, a nearby factory, which as of September 2021 was under construction, will make the new generation of sea-level Raptors while SpaceX's headquarters in California will continue building the Raptor Vacuum and test new designs.[^cite_note-Davenport-2021-425]

At Florida, a facility at Cocoa purifies [silica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon_dioxide) for Starship heat-shield tiles, producing a [slurry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slurry) that is then shipped to a facility at Cape Canaveral. In the past, workers constructed the [Starship MK2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_MK2) prototype in competition with Starbase's crews.[^cite_note-Bergeron-2021-12-76] The [Kennedy Space Center](16421.md), also in Florida, is planned to host other Starship facilities, such as a Starship launch site at [Launch Complex 39A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center_Launch_Complex_39A) and a production facility at Roberts Road. This production facility is being expanded from "Hangar X", the Falcon rocket boosters' storage and maintenance facility. It will include a 30,000 m<sup>2</sup> (320,000 ft<sup>2</sup>) building, [loading dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loading_dock), and a place for constructing integration tower sections.[^cite_note-Bergin-2022-426] Adjacent to the Kennedy Space Center will be an additional launch site at [Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Space_Launch_Complex_37), likely to service missions for the complex owner, the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force).

### Launch sites

#### Starbase



Orbital launch mount A under construction in Starbase, August 2021

Starbase hosts two launch sites, named Pad A and Pad B or Orbital Launch Pads 1 and 2 (OLP-1 and 2).[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137]<sup>: 34 </sup> A launch site at Starbase has large facilities, such as a [tank farm](3385681.md), an orbital launch mount, and an integration tower.[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137] Smaller facilities are present at the launch site: tanks surrounding the area containing methane, oxygen, nitrogen, helium, [hydraulic fluid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic_fluid), etc.;[^cite_note-FAA-2022-137]<sup>: 161 </sup> [subcoolers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcooling#In_spaceflight) near the tank farm cool propellant using liquid nitrogen; and various pipes are installed at large facilities.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] Each tank farm consists of eight tanks, enough to support one orbital launch.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The current launch mount on Pad A has a water-powered [flame diverter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_deflector), 20 clamps holding the booster, and a quick disconnect mount providing liquid fuel and electricity to the Super Heavy booster before it lifts off.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138]

The integration tower or [launch tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_structure) consists of [steel truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_truss) sections, a [lightning rod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lightning_rod) on top,[^cite_note-Berger-2021a-427] and a pair of mechanical arms that can lift, catch and recover the booster.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The decision to catch the booster with the arms was made to reduce the rocket's mass and mechanical complexity by removing the need for landing legs, as well as enabling more rapid reuse by placing the rocket directly back on the launchpad.[^cite_note-SpaceX-2023a-189]<sup>: 2 </sup> The mechanical arms are attached to a carriage and controlled by a [pulley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulley) at the top of the tower.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The pulley is linked to a [winch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winch) and [spool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_reel) at the base of the tower using a cable.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] Using the winch and the carriage, the mechanical arms can move vertically, with support from [bearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bearing_\(mechanical\)) attached at the sides of the carriage.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] A linear [hydraulic actuator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic_cylinder) moves the arms horizontally. On top of the arms are tracks, which are used to position the booster or spacecraft.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138] The tower is mounted with a quick disconnect arm extending to and contracting from the Starship spacecraft; its functions are similar to the quick disconnect mount that powers the booster.[^cite_note-Weber-2021-138]

#### Florida



Starship launch tower construction can be seen (right) at LC-39A in January 2024 as Falcon 9 launches continue to take place

SpaceX has been constructing a Starship launch pad at [Kennedy Space Center Launch Complex 39A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center_Launch_Complex_39A) (LC-39A) since 2021. The site was leased to the company in 2014 and is used to launch [Falcon 9](2647515.md) rockets.[^cite_note-Bergin-2022-426][^cite_note-Roulette-2022-428] A [Finding of No Significant Impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finding_of_No_Significant_Impact) was issued by SpaceX's [environmental impact statement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_statement) (EIS), with NASA as the lead agency, earlier in September 2019 for the launch pad site.[^cite_note-429] In 2024, the [Federal Aviation Administration](11186.md) began the process of preparing an EIS evaluating the potential impacts of the new infrastructure and a higher launch cadence of up to 44 per year at LC-39A.[^cite_note-430]

In June 2024, Blue Origin and [United Launch Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Launch_Alliance) (ULA) provided comments as part of the EIS process, both objecting to the impact that Starship launch operations may have on their own activities at the site.[^cite_note-Clark_2024-352] Blue Origin suggested several mitigations, including allowing other operators to object to a Starship launch that would conflict with one of its own, limiting Starship operations to particular times, or expanding the number of launchpads in the area to reduce the impact of conflicting launches.[^cite_note-431] ULA suggested regulators prevent Starship from launching in Florida altogether because a fully fueled Starship would require an evacuation zone so large that it would prevent other operators from using their facilities, and the noise generated by repetitive launches could be injurious to those who live or work nearby.[^cite_note-432] Elon Musk suggested that the two companies' comments were disingenuous and that their true motivation was to impede SpaceX's progress by [lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare).[^cite_note-Clark_2024-352]

The company has also proposed building another Starship launch pad at the nearby [Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 37](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Canaveral_Space_Launch_Complex_37) (SLC-37) which became vacant in 2024 after the retirement of the [Delta IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_IV) rocket. That year, the [United States Space Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Space_Force) began the process of preparing an EIS evaluating the potential impacts of new infrastructure and a launch cadence of up to 76 times per year at SLC-37.[^cite_note-Clark-2024-433] SpaceX and NASA have also worked on assessing LC-49 to the north of 39A.[^cite_note-434] The Kennedy Space Center's Master Plan One has stated that LC-49 could avoid overflight issues with pad 39B and minimize conflict with the [Canaveral National Seashore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaveral_National_Seashore).[^cite_note-435] As of January 18, 2024, NASA did not have activities underway at LC-49.[^cite_note-Nasa_2024-01-436]

Both EIS processes must be complete before SpaceX will be cleared to launch Starship from Florida, which likely will not occur until late 2025.[^cite_note-Clark_2024-352] The [Playalinda Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playalinda_Beach) has been closed by KSC Police and the National Park Service for many launches from 39A and 39B.[^cite_note-Nasa_2024-01-436] The towers and mechanical arms at the sites should be similar to the ones at Starbase.[^cite_note-Bergin-2022-426]

## Responses to Starship development

Further information: [SpaceX Starbase § Impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceX_Starbase#Impact)

In order to compete with SpaceX and close their technological gap with the company, the [China Aerospace Science and Tech Corp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Aerospace_Science_and_Technology_Corporation) and other aerospace actors in China have reportedly been working on their own equivalent of Starship—the [Long March 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_March_9) super-heavy lift rocket,[^cite_note-437] which is also designed to eventually be fully reusable.[^cite_note-NSF202304272-438] In 2021, the [China Academy of Launch Vehicle Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Academy_of_Launch_Vehicle_Technology) (CALT) showed a rendered video of a rocket noted to be "strikingly" similar to Starship in appearance and function.[^cite_note-439] In a 2022 event organized by the [International Astronautical Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronautical_Federation) and the [Chinese Society of Astronautics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Society_of_Astronautics), the CALT communicated performing research on a crewed launch vehicle powered by LOX-methane propellant, with a second stage that was very similar to Starship's.[^cite_note-440]

*[SpaceNews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SpaceNews)* noted that the Chinese start-up *Space Epoch* and engine maker [Jiuzhou Yunjian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiuzhou_Yunjian) were developing a smaller Starship-like rocket with a methane-LOX engine similar to Raptor, stainless steel tanks, and an iterative design.[^cite_note-441] Starship's reusability and stainless-steel construction might also have inspired [Project Jarvis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Jarvis), a reusable upper stage for [Blue Origin](887418.md)'s [New Glenn](51585988.md) heavy-lift launch vehicle intended to replace New Glenn's expendable upper stage in the future.[^cite_note-442]

In 2021, members of the U.S. Congress voiced concerns about the U.S. FAA's response to SpaceX's launch license violations following the explosion of SN8, calling on the FAA to "resist any potential undue influence on launch safety decision-making".[^cite_note-SpaceNews-2021-443] In 2023, prior to [Starship's second test flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starship_flight_test_2), SpaceX's vice president and ex-NASA engineer [Bill Gerstenmaier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_H._Gerstenmaier) made statements at the U.S. Senate on the importance of innovation in light of "strategic competition from state actors like China".[^cite_note-Fisher-2023-444] He said SpaceX was under a contract with NASA to use Starship to land American astronauts on the Moon before China does,[^cite_note-Bloomberg-2024-445] and that the Starship test flights campaign was being held up by "regulatory headwinds and unnecessary bureaucracy" unrelated to public safety.[^cite_note-446]

Following the second flight test of Starship, the U.S. [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) (GAO) made recommendations to the U.S. FAA to "improve its mishap investigation process", finding that historically they have allowed the launch operator to conduct their investigation with the FAA supervising.[^cite_note-Jones-2023-447] Several environmental groups have filed lawsuits against the FAA and SpaceX, claiming that environmental reviews were bypassed due to Musk's political and financial influence.[^cite_note-Fine-2024-448]
